Aromatic and clumped multi-indices: algebraic structure and Hopf embeddings
Zhicheng Zhu, Adrien Busnot Laurent

Abstract
Butcher forests extend naturally into aromatic and clumped forests and play a fundamental role in the numerical analysis of volume-preserving methods. The description of numerical volume-preservation is filled with open problems and recent attempts showed progress on specific dynamics and in low-dimension. Following this trend, we introduce aromatic and clumped multi-indices, that are simpler algebraic objects that better describe the Taylor expansions in low dimension. We provide their algebraic structure of pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra, Hopf algebroid, and Hopf algebra, and we generalise in the aromatic context the Hopf embedding from multi-indices to the BCK Hopf algebra. Keywords: multi-indices, aromatic trees, Novikov algebra, pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra, Hopf algebroid, Hopf algebra, geometric numerical integration, Butcher series. AMS subject classification (2020): 16T05, 37M15, 05C05, 16T30, 17A30.

1
footnotetext:
School of Mathematics and Statistics, Lanzhou University Lanzhou, 730000, China. zhuzhch16@lzu.edu.cn.

footnotetext:
Univ Rennes, INRIA (Research team MINGuS), IRMAR (CNRS UMR 6625) and ENS Rennes, France. Adrien.Busnot-Laurent@inria.fr.

1
Introduction

While Butcher trees were introduced for the high order analysis of Runge-Kutta methods
[
12
,
19
]
, aromatic trees are an extension that includes the use of specific graphs, in order to represent terms such as the divergence operator. They play a crucial role in the study of volume-preserving integrators
[
14
,
20
,
3
]
and are also studied for their fundamental algebraic and geometric properties
[
34
,
32
,
2
,
24
,
23
]
, as well as for other applications, for instance in stochastic numerics
[
4
]
.
They were also recently extended for the study of volume-preserving methods on manifolds
[
11
]
and yield important examples of post-Lie-Rinehart and post-Hopf algebroid structures
[
10
]
.
In particular, the standard Butcher forests and the aromatic forests have Hopf algebra structures
[
15
,
13
,
2
,
4
]
, which play a role in the creation of numerical methods, but were also used, for instance in renormalisation of QFT, or for the study of singular SPDEs. We are especially interested in the Butcher-Connes-Kreimer Hopf algebra of forests
\mathcal{H}_{BCK}
and aromatic forests
\mathcal{H}_{BCK}^{aro}
. As the algebraic structure proves challenging, the algebra of clumped forests
\mathcal{H}_{BCK}^{cl}
was later introduced in
[
4
]
.
It was observed in
[
3
]
that in fixed dimension, several degeneracies occur with aromatic trees and simpler formalisms for these fixed dimensions would help understand the full picture.
We present in this paper an algebraic object allowing to represent Taylor expansions for the study of volume-preservation in low dimension. This is a first step toward a characterisation of volume-preservation in fixed dimension.
We also mention that the question of exact volume-preservation for numerical integrators recently regained interest through its generalisation to the stochastic context. In this setting, it is shown in
[
22
,
4
]
that the design of methods for sampling exactly the invariant distribution of ergodic stochastic dynamics requires a deep understanding of so-called exotic aromatic B-series
[
25
]
. The use of multi-indices structures is a first step toward the design of such discretisations, with numerous application in stochastic optimisation, molecular dynamics, and machine learning.

The Hopf algebra of multi-indices
\mathcal{H}_{LOT}
is introduced in the work
[
30
]
for the study of rough PDEs with regularity structures.
The algebra was then further studied and extended in a variety of works including
[
29
,
5
,
21
,
28
,
9
,
6
,
7
]
.
From the numerical analysis viewpoint, multi-indices exactly correspond to the one-dimensional case: they identify the degeneracies of Butcher trees for ODEs in dimension one. Multi-indices thus serve as a toy model for tackling challenging numerical problems, before working on trees. We mention that there are no natural combinatorial structures between multi-indices and trees for representing ODEs in fixed dimension
d>1
, as pointed out in
[
8
]
.
It is shown in
[
37
]
that there exists a natural injective embedding
j
that sends the Hopf algebra of multi-indices
\mathcal{H}_{LOT}
to the Hopf algebra of trees
\mathcal{H}_{BCK}
.
This paper introduces new spaces of multi-indices analogous to aromatic and clumped forests, mimicking the concepts of numerical analysis.
We present their Hopf algebra and pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra structures. Moreover, we show that there are Hopf embeddings
j^{aro}
and
j^{cl}
between the new multi-indices spaces and the aromatic and clumped forests, of which we give an overview in the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{LOT}^{aro}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{BCK}^{aro}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{LOT}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{BCK}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{LOT}^{cl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{BCK}^{cl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{aro}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{cl}}

The paper is organised in the following way.
Section
2
presents a concise review of the algebraic structures based on aromatic trees in numerical analysis.
Section
3
presents the main results of this paper: we introduce the new aromatic and clumped multi-indices, provide their algebraic structures, and show the Hopf embeddings between multi-indices and Butcher forests.
The proofs are presented in Section
4
and rely on an aromatic extension of the Novikov algebra.

2
Preliminaries on aromatic and clumped Butcher forests

This section is devoted to a concise overview of aromatic and clumped forests and their algebraic structures, as uncovered in
[
17
,
2
,
4
]
.
We shall introduce the aromatic extensions of multi-indices by mimicking the aromatic and clumped forests in Section
3
.

2.1
The pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra of aromatic trees

Let
C
be a finite set, whose elements are called decorations (or colours in numerical analysis). We focus on trees with only one decoration for the examples for the sake of simplicity, but also as this is the case of major interest in numerics.

Definition 2.1
.

An aromatic tree is a directed graph
(V,E)
with vertices
V
and edges
E\subset V\times V
, where each vertex has exactly one outgoing edge, except one vertex called the root, that has none. The connected component with the root is called a tree, and the other connected components are called aromas. The empty aroma is denoted as
\mathbf{1}
. A multiset of aromas is called a multiaroma.
The vertices are decorated by elements of
C
, that is, we attach to each aromatic tree a map
d\colon V\rightarrow C
, often omitted in the notation for the sake of clarity.
We write
T
the vector space of trees,
A_{0}
the space of aromas,
\mathcal{A}=S(A_{0})
the space of multiaromas, understood as the symmetric algebra over aromas, and
\mathcal{A}T=\mathcal{A}\otimes T
the vector space of aromatic trees.
These spaces are naturally graded by the number of vertices, called the order of an aromatic tree
\pi
and denoted
\left|\pi\right|
.

We draw aromatic trees as follows.
Aromas are drawn in an arbitrary order on the left of the tree. By definition, each aroma has exactly one cycle, also called
K
-loop in
[
20
]
, that is, a finite set of vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{K}
such that there is an edge going from
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
,…,
v_{K}
to
v_{1}
. Thus, any aroma can be written as a tuple of trees
(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
with cyclic invariance.
The orientation of the edges goes from top to bottom and in clockwise order for cycles.
We find in particular

(2.1)

An aroma can also be described as a tuple of trees with cyclic invariance, obtained by removing the edges in the cycle of the aroma. For instance, the aroma in (
2.1

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

For clarity, we use the notation in bold
\mathbf{a}
for describing multiaromas and
a
for aromas.
Detailed combinatorics with aromatic trees are presented in
[
24
]
.

We added aromas to the standard pre-Lie structure of Butcher trees and can now wonder which structure it yields on
\mathcal{A}T
.

Definition 2.2
.

The grafting product
\curvearrowright\colon T\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
and
\curvearrowright\colon T\times T\rightarrow T
are defined by grafting the root of the first input to the nodes of the second input, summing over all vertices.
Then, the grafting product extends to aromatic trees
\curvearrowright\colon\mathcal{A}T\times\mathcal{A}T\rightarrow\mathcal{A}T
by

(a_{1}\tau_{1})\curvearrowright(a_{2}\tau_{2})=a_{1}a_{2}(\tau_{1}\curvearrowright\tau_{2})+a_{1}(\tau_{1}\curvearrowright a_{2})\tau_{2}.

The divergence
d\colon\mathcal{A}T\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
adds an edge going from the root to a node, summing over all vertices.

For instance, we find

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Remark 2.3
.

In numerical analysis, one studies numerical methods with their Taylor expansions, often represented in terms of trees
[
19
]
. For the study of methods that preserve volume, one is interested in characterising vector fields
\tilde{f}
, given by a formal Taylor expansion, that satisfy
\Div(\tilde{f})=0
. The grafting operation
\curvearrowright
on trees represents the covariant derivative and the divergence
d
on trees naturally represents the divergence operator.

The aromatic trees naturally have a structure of pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a field,
R
be a unital commutative
\mathbb{K}
-algebra, and
L
be a
R
-module, equipped with a
R
-linear map
\rho
, called the anchor map, and a map
\nabla
:

\rho:L\rightarrow Der_{\mathbb{K}}(R,R),\quad\nabla:L\rightarrow\text{End}_{\mathbf{k}}(L,L).

Then,
L
is a pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra if, with the notation
X\rhd Y:=\nabla_{X}Y
and
[X,Y]=X\rhd Y-Y\rhd X,
:

•

(L,[-,-])
is a Lie algebra over
\mathbb{K}
,

•

The anchor map
\rho
is a homomorphism of Lie algebras,

•

The Leibniz rule holds: for
f\in R
and
X,Y\in L
,
[X,fY]=\big(\rho(X)fY\big)+f[X,Y]
,

•

(L,\triangleright)
is a pre-Lie algebra: for
X,Y,Z\in L
, one has

X\rhd(Y\rhd Z)-(X\rhd Y)\rhd Z=Y\rhd(X\rhd Z)-(Y\rhd X)\rhd Z.

Proposition 2.5

(
[
17
]
)
.

The
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{A}T
is a pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra when equipped with the maps

\rho(\tau)(\mathbf{a})=\tau\curvearrowright\mathbf{a},\quad\nabla_{\tau_{1}}\tau_{2}=\tau_{1}\curvearrowright\tau_{2}.

Given a vertex
v
of an aromatic tree
\tau\in\mathcal{A}T
, let
\delta_{v}\in\text{End}(\mathcal{A}T)
be the map that grafts the root of an aromatic tree on
v
. For any choice of a vertex
v
in a rooted tree
t
, we represent
\delta_{v}(t)
by attaching a free edge (indicated by a leaf
\times

) to
v
. The space of aromatic trees with a free edge is denoted
\mathcal{A}T^{\times}
.
Then, the trace
t\colon\mathcal{A}T^{\times}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
links the root to the free edge.
In particular, we find

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The divergence then satisfies
d=t\circ\delta
, where
\delta=\sum_{v}\delta_{v}
.
Equipped with the trace, aromatic trees satisfy a universal algebraic property: the aromatic trees
(\mathcal{A}T,\curvearrowright,t)
over the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
are the free tracial pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra (see
[
17
]
for details).
We mention that aromatic structures have been extended to the post-Lie context in
[
11
,
36
]
and yield the free tracial post-Lie-Rinehart algebra.

2.2
Hopf structure of aromatic and clumped forests

In numerical analysis, Butcher trees represent vector fields and symmetric concatenation of trees, called forests, represent differential operators. The different Hopf algebra structures on Butcher forests yield important numerical results. For the analysis of splitting methods, the simpler Hopf algebras of words is used
[
1
]
, while for Runge-Kutta methods, Hopf algebras of forests are the natural approach
[
13
,
32
]
.
There are two main possibilities to build forests from aromatic trees: aromatic forests are the ones that appear in the Taylor expansions of numerical integrators, and clumped forests have a simpler algebraic structure that is useful for the formulation of backward error analysis. We refer to
[
2
,
4
]
for the details.

Definition 2.6
.

The
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{A}\otimes S(T)
is the space of aromatic forests and
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}=S(\mathcal{A}T)
is the (symmetric) algebra of clumped forests. They are graded by the number of nodes. The empty multiaroma and the empty forest are written with the same notation
\mathbf{1}
.

We use the same notation
\cdot
for all products between aromas and trees and omit it when the context is clear. We emphasize that this product is commutative.
We will use parenthesis to represent clumped forests:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}.

Let the symmetry coefficient of an aromatic forest
\pi\in\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
be the size of the automorphism group of the associated graph, where we recall that the automorphism group of a directed graph
(V,E)
is the set of permutations
g\colon V\rightarrow V
satisfying
(g\times g)(E)=E
. The symmetry coefficient on
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
is induced from the definition on
\mathcal{A}T
by

\sigma((\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})^{r_{1}}\cdots(\mathbf{a}^{n}\tau^{n})^{r_{n}})=\sigma^{ext}((\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})^{r_{1}}\cdots(\mathbf{a}^{n}\tau^{n})^{r_{n}})\sigma^{int}((\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})^{r_{1}}\cdots(\mathbf{a}^{n}\tau^{n})^{r_{n}}),

with the external and internal symmetry factors:

\displaystyle\sigma^{ext}((\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})^{r_{1}}\cdots(\mathbf{a}^{n}\tau^{n})^{r_{n}})

\displaystyle=r_{1}!\cdots r_{n}!,

\displaystyle\sigma^{int}((\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})^{r_{1}}\cdots(\mathbf{a}^{n}\tau^{n})^{r_{n}})

\displaystyle=\sigma(\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})^{r_{1}}\cdots\sigma(\mathbf{a}^{n}\tau^{n})^{r_{n}}.

We find

\sigma(\hbox to14.67pt{\vbox to20.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.28337pt\lower-3.33301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
\par{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})=2.

We observe that
\sigma(\mathbf{a}\tau)=\sigma(\mathbf{a})\sigma(\tau)
for
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
,
\tau\in T
, but we emphasize that
\sigma(\mathbf{a})
is not the product of the symmetry coefficients of each aroma in
\mathbf{a}
.
Let the projection
\psi\colon\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
be given by

\psi\colon(\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})\dots(\mathbf{a}^{m}\tau^{m})\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}\mapsto\mathbf{a}^{1}\dots\mathbf{a}^{m}\tau^{1}\dots\tau^{m}\in\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}.

Note that
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
cannot be injected straightforwardly to
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
as the multiaromas alone
\mathcal{A}
do not appear in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
.
Let the inner product
\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle=\sigma(\pi_{1})\mathds{1}_{\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}}
and the associated dual map of
\psi
:

\psi^{*}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F},\quad\langle\psi(\pi_{1}),\pi_{2}\rangle=\langle\pi_{1},\psi^{*}(\pi_{2})\rangle.

For instance, we find

\psi^{*}(\hbox to22.63pt{\vbox to12.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.28337pt\lower-3.33301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
\par{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}

{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 12.13 1.94 L 12.13 9.08}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}).

The pre-Lie grafting product
\curvearrowright
extends to
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
by the standard Guin-Oudom procedure
[
35
]
.
The Grossman-Larson product
\star
is implicitly defined on both clumped and aromatic forests by

(\pi_{1}\star\pi_{2})\curvearrowright\pi_{3}=\pi_{1}\curvearrowright(\pi_{2}\curvearrowright\pi_{3}).

Then,
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
is equipped with the coproduct
\Delta a\pi=\Delta a\cdot\Delta\pi
, where we use the standard deshuffle coproduct on the symmetric algebras
\mathcal{A}=S(A^{0})
and
S(T)
.
We find for instance:

On clumped forests, we consider the standard coproduct
\Delta
on the symmetric algebras

We naturally obtain Hopf algebra structures, but also Hopf algebroid structures for the aromatic forests (that are out of the scope of the present paper, see
[
31
,
33
]
).

Proposition 2.7
.

The spaces
(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F},\cdot,\Delta)
,
(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F},\star,\Delta)
,
(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F},\cdot,\Delta)
, and
(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F},\star,\Delta)
are Hopf algebras.

Let us extend the celebrated Butcher-Connes-Kreimer coproduct
[
15
,
16
]
to
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 2.8
.

An admissible cut
c
of a tree
\tau\in T
is a possibly empty subset of the edges of
\tau
where any path from the root to a leaf of
x
has at most one cut.
An admissible cut of an aroma
a=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{K})\in A^{0}
, written as a tuple with cyclic invariance, is the union of admissible cuts of the
\tau_{k}
. In other words, an admissible cut of an aroma cannot cut the edges in the cycle.
If
c
is admissible, the connected components obtained by removing the edges in
c
are collected in
R^{c}(x)
that contains the root or the cycle, and
P^{c}(x)
for the other components.

Definition 2.9
.

The Butcher-Connes-Kreimer coproduct on
A^{0}
and
T
is defined by the extraction contraction formula

\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}x=x\otimes\mathbf{1}+\sum_{c\in Adm(x)}P^{c}(x)\otimes R^{c}(x),

The coproduct is extended into
\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
as a morphism:

\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}a^{1}\cdots a^{m}\tau^{1}\cdots\tau^{n}=\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}a^{1}\cdots\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}a^{m}\cdot\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}\tau^{1}\cdots\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}\tau^{n}.

On clumped forests, the clumped BCK coproduct is defined for
\mathbf{a}\tau,\mathbf{a}_{1}\tau_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{n}\tau_{n}\in\mathcal{A}T
by

\Delta_{BCK}^{cl}\tau=(\psi^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\circ\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}(\tau),\quad\Delta_{BCK}^{cl}(\mathbf{a}_{1}\tau_{1})\cdots(\mathbf{a}_{n}\tau_{n})=\Delta_{BCK}^{cl}\mathbf{a}_{1}\tau_{1}\cdots\Delta_{BCK}^{cl}\mathbf{a}_{n}\tau_{n}.

Example 2.10
.

Consider the aromatic tree
\tau=\hbox to24.23pt{\vbox to20.89pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.28337pt\lower-3.33301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
\par{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}
{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 11.02 C -3.04 11.02 -5.51 8.56 -5.51 5.51 C -5.51 2.47 -3.04 0 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{}{}{}{}}}{{}{}{}{}}}}{}
{}
{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 11.02 C 3.04 11.02 5.51 8.56 5.51 5.51 C 5.51 2.47 3.04 0 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{
{}{}{}}}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

A more involved example is detailed in Table
1
and further examples are presented in the appendices of
[
2
,
4
]
.

Table 1
:
Admissible cuts and related maps for an aroma.

\text{Cut }c

\mathbf{1}

Theorem 2.11

(
[
2
,
4
]
)
.

The space
(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F},\cdot,\Delta_{BCK}^{aro})
is a Hopf algebra and is isomorphic (up to the symmetry coefficient) to the dual of the Grossman-Larson Hopf algebra
(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F},\star,\Delta)
, with
\Delta
the standard deshuffle coproduct.
Analogously, the space
(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F},\cdot,\Delta_{BCK}^{cl})
is a Hopf algebra and is isomorphic (up to the symmetry coefficient) to the dual of the Grossman-Larson Hopf algebra
(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F},\star,\Delta)
.

Remark 2.12
.

More generally, the aromatic/clumped forests can be equipped with a structure of pre-Hopf algebroid/algebra
[
4
]
. We mention that planar extensions of these objects exist and yield post-Hopf algebroid/algebra
[
27
,
10
,
11
]
.

Remark 2.13
.

The BCK structure allows to formulate the composition of differential operators given by a Taylor expansion (more precisely, by S-series). The product dual to the BCK coproduct is called the composition law and appears in the order analysis of numerical integrators of Runge-Kutta type.
The use of Hopf structures with aromatic and clumped forests allows in particular the explicit description of the Taylor expansion of methods that preserve volume and the analysis of aromatic B-series methods
[
34
,
2
,
24
]
.

3
Aromatic and clumped multi-indices

In this section, we introduce the new objects of aromatic and clumped multi-indices, generalising
[
26
,
30
]
, and we present the main results of this paper. More precisely, we uncover the pre-Lie-Rinehart and Hopf algebraic structures of the new multi-indices and derive Hopf embeddings from mutiindices to their tree counterparts, generalising
[
37
]
.

3.1
The free aromatic Novikov algebra

A Novikov algebra is a vector space
N
over a base field
\mathbb{K}
, together with a bilinear product
\rhd:N\times N\rightarrow N
such that, for any
x,y,z\in N
, the following identities hold:

\displaystyle x\rhd(y\rhd z)-(x\rhd y)\rhd z

\displaystyle=y\rhd(x\rhd z)-(y\rhd x)\rhd z,

(3.1)

\displaystyle(x\rhd y)\rhd z

\displaystyle=(x\rhd z)\rhd y.

(3.2)

The equation (
3.1
) is the pre-Lie identity, while the equation (
3.2
) is the right-NAP identity (for non associative permutative). We denote the free Novikov algebra generated by the set
C
by
N(C)
.
This is described as follows: let
\overline{N}(C)
be the commutative algebra of polynomials with variables
x_{j}^{a},(a,j)\in C\times\{-1,0,1,2,\cdots\}
, let
\partial
be the unique derivation of
\overline{N}(C)
such that
\partial x_{j}^{a}=x_{j+1}^{a}
.

A basis of
\overline{N}(C)
is given by the monomials

x^{\mathbf{k}}\colon=\prod_{j\geq-1,a\in C}(x_{j}^{a})^{k_{j}^{a}},

and the weight of
x^{\mathbf{k}}
is defined as

\mathbf{wt}(x^{\mathbf{k}})=\sum_{a\in C,j\leq-1}jk_{j}^{a}

where the exponents
k_{j}^{a}
are non-negative integers, and all terms of the product vanish except a finite number of them. The weight induces a unique
\mathbb{Z}
-grading of the algebra
\overline{N}(C)
, for which the derivation
\partial
is homogeneous of degree one. The derivation
\partial
satisfies

\partial x^{\mathbf{k}}=\sum_{j\geq-1,a\in C}k_{j}^{a}x^{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{e}_{j}^{a}+\mathbf{e}_{j+1}^{a}}

(3.3)

where
\mathbf{e}_{j}^{a}
is the multi-index whose entries are all zero except for a single non-zero entry in position
(j,a)
. In other words,
x^{\mathbf{e}_{j}^{a}}=x_{j}^{a}
. Let
M_{n}
be the monoid of weight
n
and the bilinear product
P\rhd Q\colon=P\cdot\partial Q
. Then
(M_{-1},\rhd)
is a pre-Lie algebra.

In the spirit of aromatic trees, we construct the aromatic Novikov algebra by
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}:=S(M_{0})\otimes M_{-1}
, where
S(V)
denotes the symmetric tensorial algebra. Aromas are in
M_{0}
, multiaromas in
S(M_{0})
, and
M_{-1}
represents the analogue of trees.
The multi-indices analogues of aromatic and clumped forests are
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}=S(M_{0})\otimes S(M_{-1})
and
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}=S(\mathcal{A}M_{-1})
.
For the sake of simplicity, we use the same notation
\odot
for the products in
S(M_{0})
, in
S(M_{-1})
, and in
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
. Moreover, the unit of all symmetric tensorial algebras is denoted as
\mathbf{1}
.
The product of the symmetric algebra
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}=S(\mathcal{A}M_{-1})
is denoted by
\diamond
.
Aromatic monomials
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
are spanned by elements of the form

x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}},\quad x^{\kappa^{i}}\in M_{0},\quad x^{\mathbf{k}^{i}}\in M_{-1}.

On the other hand, clumped monomials are spanned by elements of the form

(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\diamond\cdots\diamond(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}),\quad\mathbf{y}^{i}\in S(M_{0}),\quad x^{\mathbf{k}^{i}}\in M_{-1}.

The weight is defined on aromatic monomials by

\mathbf{wt}(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{wt}(x^{\kappa^{i}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{wt}(x^{\mathbf{k}^{i}}),

and is then extended on clumped monomials by

\mathbf{wt}((\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\diamond\cdots\diamond(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{wt}(\mathbf{y}^{i}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{i}}).

Note that the weight on monomials in
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
is
-1
as expected.
The symmetry factor on
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
is given by,

\sigma((x^{\kappa^{1}})^{\odot q_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot(x^{\kappa^{m}})^{\odot q_{m}}\odot(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})^{\odot p_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot(x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})^{\odot p_{n}})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}q_{j}!(\kappa^{j}!)^{q_{j}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}!(\mathbf{k}^{i}!)^{p_{i}}.

Then, we have on clumped monomials

\sigma((\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})^{\diamond p_{1}}\diamond\cdots\diamond(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})^{\diamond p_{n}})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}!\sigma(\mathbf{y}^{i}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{i}})^{p_{i}}.

The degree of an element of
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
is

\deg(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{a\in C,j\geq-1}\kappa_{j}^{i,a}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{a\in C,j\geq-1}\mathbf{k}_{j}^{i,a},

and analogously on
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
.
The pairing on
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
(respectively
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
) is given by

\langle\mathbb{M},\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\rangle:=\sigma(\mathbb{M})\mathds{1}_{\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}^{\prime}},

where we use the symmetry coefficient on
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
(respectively
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
).

Let us now extend the product
\rhd
.
The bilinear product
\rhd\colon\mathcal{A}M_{-1}\times M_{n}
for
n=-1,0
is defined by
(\mathbf{y}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})\rhd P=\mathbf{y}\odot(x^{\mathbf{k}}\cdot\partial P)
.
Then,
\rhd
extends on
S(M_{0})
and
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
by the Leibniz rule:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})\rhd(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\kappa}\odot\cdots\odot(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\rhd x^{\kappa^{j}})\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\rhd(\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\rhd\mathbf{y}^{2})\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}}+\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\rhd x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}})

As
\rhd
is defined as a derivation, the aromatic Novikov algebra
(\mathcal{A}M_{-1},\rhd)
naturally satisfies the identities (
3.1
) and (
3.2
), and thus is a pre-Lie algebra.
More precisely, we show that the aromatic Novikov algebra
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
is equipped with a pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra structure.

Proposition 3.1
.

The aromatic Novikov algebra
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}=S(M_{0})\otimes M_{-1}
is a pre-Lie-Rinehart algebra for the product

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}M_{-1}\times\mathcal{A}M_{-1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}M_{-1}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}},\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}})

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\rhd(\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}})

and the induced maps:

\displaystyle\nabla:\mathcal{A}M_{-1}\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathcal{A}M_{-1},\mathcal{A}M_{1})

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\mapsto\big(\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}}\mapsto(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\rhd(\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}})\big)

\displaystyle\rho:\mathcal{A}M_{-1}\rightarrow\text{Der}_{\mathbf{k}}(S(M_{0}),S(M_{0}))

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\mapsto\big(\mathbf{y}^{2}\mapsto(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\rhd\mathbf{y}^{2}\big)

where the space
\text{Der}_{\mathbf{k}}(S(M_{0}),S(M_{0}))
collects all the derivation maps on
S(M_{0})
and
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
is a Lie algebra for the Lie bracket
[x,y]=x\rhd y-y\rhd x
.

Proof.

Let us check that the map
\rho
satisfies the Leibniz rule: for
\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}
,
\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}}\in\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
and
\mathbf{y}\in S(M_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle[\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}},\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}}]

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\rhd(\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}})-(\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}})\rhd(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\partial(\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}})-\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}}\odot\partial(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})

\displaystyle+\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\partial(\mathbf{y})\odot\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}\odot[\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}},\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}}]+\rho(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})(\mathbf{y})\mathbf{y}^{2}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{2}}.

One shows similarly that
[-,-]
is a Lie bracket.

\square

Remark 3.2
.

Following
[
18
]
, the product
\rhd
can be seen as a connection on
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
with vanishing torsion and curvature. The multiaromas represent the functions, while the aromatic Novikov algebra contains the analogues of vector fields.

3.2
The Hopf algebras of aromatic and clumped monomials

Our aim is to extend the LOT Hopf algebra
[
30
,
37
]
to the aromatic monomials. We recall that the standard LOT Hopf algebra generated by a set
C
is
\mathcal{H}_{LOT}^{C}=\big(S(N(C)),\star,\Delta,\mathds{1},\epsilon\big)^{\circ}
, that is, the graded dual of the Hopf algebra
\big(S(N(C)),\star,\Delta,\mathds{1},\epsilon\big)
, equipped with the Grossman-Larson product
\star
and the deshuffle coproduct
\Delta
. This structure is analogous to the BCK Hopf algebra of Butcher trees
[
15
]
for multi-indices. We extend here the LOT structure to the aromatic context.

We provide a definition of the aromatic LOT coproduct using free edges and cuts in Section
4
.
We will then provide a straightforward explicit formula for the coproduct, that we give here directly for the sake of simplicity. The proof of this theorem is postponed to Section
4.3
.

Theorem 3.3
.

The aromatic LOT coproduct
\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}:\mathcal{A}M_{-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
is the unique unital morphism defined by

\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(\mathbf{y}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\kappa_{1}})\odot\cdots\odot\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\kappa_{m}})\odot\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\mathbf{k}})

(3.4)

where
\mathbf{y}=x^{\kappa_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa_{m}}
, the coproduct on aromas
M_{0}
and monomials
M_{-1}
is

\displaystyle\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\kappa})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0}\sum_{\underset{\mathbf{wt}(x^{\kappa^{i}})=-1}{\kappa=\kappa^{1}+\cdots+\kappa^{t}+\overline{\kappa}}}\frac{\kappa!}{\sigma(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{t}})\overline{\kappa}!}x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{t}}\otimes\overline{\partial}^{t}x^{\overline{\kappa}}+x^{\kappa}\otimes\mathbf{1},

\displaystyle\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\mathbf{k}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\geq 0}\sum_{\underset{\mathbf{wt}(x^{\mathbf{k}^{i}})=-1}{\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{k}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{k}^{r}+\overline{\mathbf{k}}}}\frac{\mathbf{k}!}{\sigma(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{r}})\overline{\mathbf{k}}!}x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{r}}\otimes\overline{\partial}^{r}x^{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}+x^{\mathbf{k}}\otimes\mathbf{1},

and
\overline{\partial}
is explictly given in Proposition
4.4
.

Let us consider the projection
\varphi:\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
, defined by

\varphi:(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\diamond\cdots\diamond(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}\longmapsto\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot\cdots\odot\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}\in\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}.

Since the multi-aromas alone
S(M_{0})
do not appear in
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
, the algebra
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
does not inject straightforwardly into
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
.
Let
\varphi^{*}:\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
be the dual of
\varphi
for the inner product
\langle-,-\rangle
, that is,

\langle\varphi(\mathbb{M}),\mathbb{M}^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\mathbb{M},\varphi^{*}(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})\rangle.

The LOT coproduct on aromatic monomials extends straightforwardly as a morphism for
\diamond
as

\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
.
Then, the LOT coproduct on clumped monomials is for
\pi_{cl}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
:

\Delta_{LOT}^{cl}=(\varphi^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\circ\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}.

The LOT structures on aromatic and clumped monomials yield analogous Hopf structures to the ones of aromatic forests
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
and clumped forests
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
. The proof is analogous to the one on tree structures from
[
4
]
.

Proposition 3.4
.

The aromatic multi-indices
(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M},\odot,\Delta_{LOT}^{aro})
and the clumped multi-indices
(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M},\diamond,\Delta_{LOT}^{cl})
equipped with the LOT coproduct are Hopf algebras.

3.3
Hopf embedding between multi-indices and forests

In this section, we present the main result of this paper, that is, that there exist Hopf embeddings
j^{aro}
and
j^{cl}
between aromatic/clumped monomials and their trees counterpart.

The Hopf embeddings are based on the fertility map
\Phi
, which is defined as follows.

Definition 3.5
.

Let the fertility map
\Phi
be given by

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle a^{1}\cdots a^{n}t

\displaystyle\mapsto\Phi(a^{1})\odot\cdots\odot\Phi(a^{n})\odot\Phi(t)

where

\Phi(t)=\prod_{v\in V(t)}x_{f(v)-1}^{d(v)},\quad\Phi(a^{j})=\prod_{v\in V(a^{j})}x_{f(v)-1}^{d(v)},

and
d(v)
and
f(v)
are the colour and the fertility of the vertex
v
.
The fertility map
\Phi
is defined on
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
as a morphism:

\Phi\big((\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})\cdots(\mathbf{a}^{n}\tau^{n})\big)=\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{1}\tau^{1})\diamond\cdots\diamond\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{n}\tau^{n}).

Theorem 3.6
.

The fertility map induces a pre-Lie-Rinehart morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{A}\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
.

Proof.

By construction, the map
\Phi:(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{T},\curvearrowright)\rightarrow(\mathcal{A}M_{-1},\rhd)
is a pre-Lie algebra morphism.
Then, the map
\Phi
satisfies

\displaystyle\Phi([\mathbf{a}^{1}t^{1},\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{2}t^{2}])

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{1}t^{1}\curvearrowright\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{2}t^{2}-\mathbf{a}\mathbf{a}^{2}t^{2}\curvearrowright\mathbf{a}^{1}t^{1})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{1}t^{1})\rhd(\Phi(\mathbf{a})\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{2}t^{2}))-\Phi(\mathbf{a})\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{2}t^{2})\rhd\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{1}t^{1})

\displaystyle=\Phi(\mathbf{a})[\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{1}t^{1}),\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{2}t^{2})]+\rho(\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{1}t^{1}))(\Phi(\mathbf{a}))\Phi(\mathbf{a}^{2}t^{2})).

Hence the result.

\square

Definition 3.7
.

Let
j^{aro}
be the dual map to
\Phi
on
M_{0}
and
M_{-1}
, that is,

\langle\Phi(a),x^{\kappa}\rangle=\langle a,j^{aro}(x^{\kappa})\rangle,\quad\langle\Phi(t),\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{k}}\rangle=\langle t,j^{aro}(x^{\mathbf{k}})\rangle.

We extend
j^{aro}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
as a morphism

j^{aro}\big(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}\big)=j^{aro}(x^{\kappa^{1}})\cdots j^{aro}(x^{\kappa^{m}})j^{aro}(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\cdots j^{aro}(x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}).

The analogue
j^{cl}\colon\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
on clumped monomials is

j^{cl}\big((\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\diamond\cdots\diamond(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})\big)=j^{aro}(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\cdots j^{aro}(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}).

The following formula is deduced straightforwardly from the definition. Note that it does not extend in general to
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

The maps
j^{aro}
satisfies

j^{aro}(x^{\kappa})=\sum_{\Phi(a)=x^{\kappa}}\frac{\sigma(x^{\kappa})}{\sigma(a)}a,\quad j^{aro}(x^{\mathbf{k}})=\sum_{\Phi(t)=x^{\mathbf{k}}}\frac{\sigma(x^{\mathbf{k}})}{\sigma(t)}t.

(3.5)

For example, one finds:

\displaystyle j^{aro}(x_{0}x_{-1})=\hbox to3.24pt{\vbox to11.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.61987pt\lower-1.61987pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{

Definition 3.9
.

The symmetry factor of aroma forest is the cardinal of its automorphism group, then for multi-aromas
\mathbf{a}=(a^{1})^{\odot\ell_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot(a^{n})^{\odot\ell_{n}}
, where
a^{1},\ldots,a^{n}
are pairwise distinct aromas, the symmetry factor can be expressed by its distinct aroma components

\displaystyle\sigma(a)=\ell_{1}!\cdots\ell_{n}!\sigma(a^{1})^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\sigma(a^{n})^{\ell_{n}},

here the symmetry factor
\sigma(a)
is obtained by external symmetry factor
\sigma^{ext}(\mathbf{a}):=\ell_{1}!\cdots\ell_{n}!
by the internal symmetry factor
\sigma^{int}(a)=\sigma(a^{1})^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\sigma(a^{n})^{\ell_{n}}
, futhermore for an element
\mathbf{a}\mathbf{\tau}\in\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}
with
\mathbf{a}=(a^{1})^{\odot\ell_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot(a^{n})^{\ell_{n}}
and
\tau=(\tau^{1})^{\odot q_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot(\tau^{m})^{\odot q_{m}}
. The external symmetry factor is
\sigma^{ext}(\mathbf{a}\tau)=\ell_{1}!\cdots\ell_{n}!q_{1}!\cdots q_{m}!
and internal symmetry factor
\sigma^{int}(\mathbf{a}\tau)=\sigma(a^{1})^{\ell_{1}}\cdots\sigma(a^{n})^{\ell_{n}}\sigma(\tau^{1})^{q_{1}}\cdots\sigma(\tau^{m})^{q_{m}}.

The maps
j^{aro}
and
j^{cl}
are Hopf embeddings from the multi-indices algebras to their tree counterparts, as shown in the following result, whose proof is postponed to Section
4
.

Theorem 3.10
.

The map
j^{aro}
(respectively
j^{cl}
) yields the following Hopf algebroid embedding (respectively Hopf algebra embedding):

\displaystyle j^{aro}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{H}_{LOT}^{aro}=\big(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M},\cdot,\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}\big)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{BCK}^{aro}=\big(\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F},\cdot,\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}\big),

\displaystyle j^{cl}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{H}_{LOT}^{cl}=\big(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M},\cdot,\Delta_{LOT}^{cl}\big)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{BCK}^{cl}=\big(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F},\cdot,\Delta_{BCK}^{cl}\big).

In addition, the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{LOT}^{aro}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{BCK}^{aro}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{LOT}^{cl}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{BCK}^{cl}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{aro}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{cl}}

4
Free edges, graftings, cuts, and Hopf embeddings

This section defines the necessary concepts (free edges, graftings,…) and use them for proving the Hopf embeddings of Theorem
3.3
and the explicit description of the aromatic LOT coproduct.

4.1
Free edges and extended maps

Similar to the free Novikov algebra which is embedded in the Novikov algebra, we define the aromatic and clumped equivalent of such notions.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\overline{A_{0}}
and
\overline{T}
be the spaces of aromas and trees with free edges given by

\overline{A_{0}}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}A_{0}^{(n)},\quad\overline{T}=\bigoplus_{n}^{\infty}T^{(n)},

where
A_{0}^{(n)}
and
T^{(n)}
are the spaces of aromas with
n
free edges and space of rooted trees with
n
free edges. Then we define the space of aromatic trees with free edges by

\overline{\mathcal{A}T}=S(\overline{A_{0}})\otimes\overline{T}.

The weight is extended as the total number of the edges minus the number of vertices for tree-like structures.
Analogously, let the aromatic multi-indices with higher weight,

\overline{M_{-1}}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}M_{-1}^{(n)},\quad\overline{M_{0}}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}M_{0}^{(n)},

where
M_{p}^{(n)}\simeq M_{n+p}
is the space of weight
n+p
with respect to the original space
M_{p}
and
p=0,1
. Furthermore, we define the aromatic Novikov algebra with higher weight:

\overline{\mathcal{A}M_{-1}}=S(\overline{M_{0}})\otimes\overline{M_{-1}}.

Example 4.2
.

The monomials
x_{0}^{3}x_{2}\in\overline{M_{0}}
and
x_{0}^{3}x_{2}\in\overline{M_{-1}}
respectively correspond to the following aroma and tree with free edges:

\hbox to16.53pt{\vbox to35.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.2501pt\lower-3.33301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{
\par{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.51 44.09}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.51 44.09 L -11.02 33.07}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}}{{{

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 12.85 11.02 C 12.85 12.03 12.03 12.85 11.02 12.85 C 10.01 12.85 9.2 12.03 9.2 11.02 C 9.2 10.01 10.01 9.2 11.02 9.2 C 12.03 9.2 12.85 10.01 12.85 11.02 Z M 11.02 11.02}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

We emphasize that the monomials are enforced to be different as they belong to two different spaces.

As
\partial\colon M_{n}\rightarrow M_{n+1}
, the derivation induces naturally the maps

\partial\colon\overline{M_{0}}\rightarrow\overline{M_{0}},\quad\partial\colon\overline{M_{-1}}\rightarrow\overline{M_{-1}}.

The derivation
\partial
is extended on
\overline{\mathcal{A}M_{-1}}
as a derivation:

\partial(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot\partial x^{\kappa^{j}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}}+x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot\partial x^{\mathbf{k}}.

Analogously to (
3.3
), let the transpose derivation on
M_{n}
:

\overline{\partial}x^{\mathbf{k}}=\sum_{j\geq 0,a\in C}k_{j}^{a}x^{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{e}_{j}^{a}+\mathbf{e}_{j-1}^{a}}.

While
\partial
increases the weight,
\overline{\partial}
decreases it.
We extend
\overline{\partial}
to
\overline{M_{0}}
,
\overline{M_{-1}}
, and
\overline{\mathcal{A}M_{-1}}
as before.

Proposition 4.3
.

The transpose derivation
\overline{\partial}
is the transpose of the derivation
\partial
, that is, for
P,Q\in\overline{M_{0}}
or
P,Q\in\overline{M_{-1}}
,

\langle\partial P,Q\rangle=\langle P,\overline{\partial}Q\rangle.

Proof.

We already have the result on
\overline{M_{-1}}
from
[
37
]
.
Then, for
x^{\mu},x^{\nu}\in\overline{M_{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\partial x^{\mu},x^{\nu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0,a\in C}\mu_{i}^{a}\langle x^{\mu-e_{t}^{a}+e_{t+1}^{a}},x^{\nu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0,a\in C}\mu_{i}^{a}\nu!\mathds{1}_{(\mu-e_{t}^{a}+e_{t+1}^{a}=\nu)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0,a\in C}(\nu+e_{t}^{a})!\mathds{1}_{(\mu-e_{t}^{a}+e_{t+1}^{a}=\nu)}.

On the other hand, we find

\displaystyle\langle x^{\mu},\overline{\partial}x^{\nu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 1,a\in C}\nu_{t}^{a}\langle x^{\mu},x^{\nu-e_{t}^{a}+e_{t-1}^{a}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 1,a\in C}\nu_{t}^{a}(\nu-e_{t}^{a}+e_{t-1}^{a})!\mathds{1}_{(\nu-e_{t}^{a}+e_{t-1}^{a}=\mu)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 1,a\in C}(\nu+e_{t-1}^{a})!\mathds{1}_{(\nu-e_{t}^{a}+e_{t-1}^{a}=\mu)}.

Hence the result.

\square

An explicit expression for the iterated transpose derivation is given by the following result.

Proposition 4.4
.

For any multi-indices
\kappa,\mathbf{k}
, denote their corresponding left-shifted multi-indices by
\mathop{\kappa}^{\leftarrow}\limits,\mathop{\mathbf{k}}^{\leftarrow}\limits
with coordinates
\mathop{\kappa_{t}^{a}}^{\leftarrow}\limits:=\kappa_{t+1}^{a}
.
For any integer
r\geq 0
, the following formula holds

\overline{\partial}^{r}(\mathbf{y}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=\sum_{|\ell^{1}|+\cdots+|\ell^{n}|+|\ell|=r}C_{\kappa^{1},\ell^{1}}\cdots C_{\kappa^{n},\ell^{n}}C_{k,\ell}x^{\kappa^{1}-\ell^{1}+\mathop{\ell^{1}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{n}-\ell^{n}+\mathop{\ell^{n}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}-\ell+\mathop{\ell}^{\leftarrow}\limits},

where
\mathbf{y}=x^{\kappa_{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa_{n}}
and the coefficient
C_{\kappa^{i},\ell^{i}},C_{\mathbf{k},\ell}
are given by
C_{\kappa^{i},0}=C_{\mathbf{k},0}=0
for
1\leq i\leq n
and the recursive formula below:

\displaystyle C_{\mathbf{k},\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq-1,a\in C,\ell_{t}\geq 1}C_{\mathbf{k},\ell-\mathbf{e}_{t}^{a}}(\mathbf{k}_{t}^{a}-\ell_{t}+1+\ell_{t+1}^{a})

\displaystyle C_{\kappa^{i},\ell^{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t^{\prime}\geq 0,a\in C,\ell^{i}_{t^{\prime}}\geq 1}C_{\kappa^{i},\ell^{i}-\mathbf{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{a}}(\kappa^{i,a}_{t^{\prime}}-\ell^{i}_{t^{\prime}}+1+\ell^{i,a}_{t^{\prime}+1})

Proof.

The definition of
\overline{\partial}
immediately yields

C_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{e}_{t}^{a}}=\mathbf{k}_{t}^{a},\quad C_{\mathbf{\kappa}^{i},\mathbf{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{a}}=\kappa^{i,a}_{t^{\prime}}.

We therefore obtain

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}^{r+1}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{y}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=\overline{\partial}\hskip-17.07164pt\sum_{|\ell^{1^{\prime}}|+\cdots+|\ell^{n^{\prime}}|+|\ell^{\prime}|=r}C_{\kappa^{1},\ell^{1^{\prime}}}\cdots C_{\kappa^{n},\ell^{n^{\prime}}}C_{\mathbf{k},\ell}x^{\kappa^{1}-\ell^{1^{\prime}}+\mathop{\ell^{1^{\prime}}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{n}-\ell^{n^{\prime}}+\mathop{\ell^{n^{\prime}}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}-\ell+\mathop{\ell}^{\leftarrow}\limits}

\displaystyle=\hskip-17.07164pt\sum_{|\ell^{1^{\prime}}|+\cdots+|\ell^{n^{\prime}}|+|\ell^{\prime}|=r}\sum_{\ell^{i^{\prime}}+\mathbf{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{a}=\ell^{i}}C_{\kappa^{1},\ell^{1^{\prime}}}x^{\kappa^{1}-\ell^{1}+\mathop{\ell^{1}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}\odot\cdots\odot C_{\kappa^{i},\ell^{i^{\prime}}}(\kappa_{t^{\prime}}^{a}+\ell_{t^{\prime}+1}^{i,a}-\ell_{t^{\prime}}^{i^{\prime},a})

\displaystyle\odot x^{\kappa^{i}+\mathop{(\ell^{i^{\prime}}+\mathbf{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{a})}^{\leftarrow}\limits-(\ell^{i^{\prime}}+\mathbf{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{a})}\odot\cdots\odot C_{\kappa^{n},\ell^{n}}x^{\kappa^{n}-\ell^{n}+\mathop{\ell^{n}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}-\ell+\mathop{\ell}^{\leftarrow}\limits}

\displaystyle+\sum_{|\ell^{1^{\prime}}|+\cdots+|\ell^{n^{\prime}}|+|\ell^{\prime}|=r}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{t}^{a}=\ell}C_{\kappa^{1},\ell^{1}}\cdots C_{\kappa^{n},\ell^{n}}x^{\kappa^{1}-\ell^{1}+\mathop{\ell^{1}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{n}-\ell^{n}+\mathop{\ell^{n}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}

\displaystyle\odot C_{\mathbf{k},\ell^{\prime}}(k_{t}^{a}+\ell_{t+1}^{a}-\ell^{{}^{\prime}a}_{t})x^{\mathbf{k}+\mathop{(\ell^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{t}^{a})}^{\leftarrow}\limits-(\ell^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{t}^{a})}

\displaystyle=\hskip-17.07164pt\sum_{|\ell^{1}|+\cdots+|\ell^{n}|+|\ell|=r+1}\sum_{(\ell^{i^{\prime}},t^{\prime},a),\ell^{i^{\prime}}+\mathbf{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{a}=\ell^{i}}C_{\kappa^{1},\ell^{1}}x^{\kappa^{1}+\mathop{\ell^{1}}^{\leftarrow}\limits-\ell^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot C_{\kappa^{i},\ell^{i}-\mathbf{e}_{t^{\prime}}^{a}}(\kappa_{t^{\prime}}^{a}+\ell_{t^{\prime}+1}^{a}-\ell_{t^{\prime}}^{a}+1)

\displaystyle\odot x^{\kappa^{i}+\mathop{\ell^{i}}^{\leftarrow}\limits-\ell^{i}}\odot\cdots\odot C_{\kappa^{n},\ell^{n}}x^{\kappa^{n}+\mathop{\ell^{n}}^{\leftarrow}\limits-\ell^{n}}\odot C_{\mathbf{k},\ell}x^{\mathbf{k}+\mathop{\ell}^{\leftarrow}\limits-\ell}

\displaystyle+\hskip-17.07164pt\sum_{|\ell^{1}|+\cdots+|\ell^{n}|+|\ell|=r+1}\sum_{(\ell^{\prime},t,a),\ell^{\prime}+\mathbf{e}_{t}^{a}=\ell}C_{\kappa^{1},\ell^{1}}\cdots C_{\kappa^{n},\ell^{n}}x^{\kappa^{1}-\ell^{1}+\mathop{\ell^{1}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{n}-\ell^{n}+\mathop{\ell^{n}}^{\leftarrow}\limits}

\displaystyle\odot C_{\mathbf{k},\ell-\mathbf{e}_{t}^{a}}(\mathbf{k}_{t}^{a}+\ell_{t+1}^{a}-\ell_{t}^{a}+1)x^{\mathbf{k}+\mathop{\ell}^{\leftarrow}\limits-\ell}

Hence the result.

\square

The following result is a universal combinatorial property applied in our context of multi-indices and tree-like structures.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\mathbb{M}=M_{1}\diamond\cdots\diamond M_{r}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{M}
and
\mathbb{F}\in\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}
such that
\Phi(\mathbb{F})=\mathbb{M}
.
Let
\mathcal{B}
be the set of
r
-tuples of aromatic trees given by

\mathcal{B}:=\{(\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{r}),\tau_{1}\cdots\tau_{r}=\mathbb{F}\ and\ \Phi(M_{j})=\tau_{j}\ for\ any\ j=1,\cdots,r\}.

Then, one finds

|\mathcal{B}|=\frac{\sigma^{ext}(\mathbb{M})}{\sigma^{ext}(\mathbb{F})}.

Proof.

The external automorphism group of
\mathbb{M}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{B}
. The stabiliser of the
r
-tuple
(t_{1},\cdots,t_{r})
is the external automorphism group of the forest
F
. One concludes by the orbit-stabiliser theorem.

\square

Let
\delta:\overline{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}}
be given by

\delta(a\tau)=\sum_{v\in V(a\tau)}\delta_{v}(a\tau),

where
\delta_{v}
adds a free edge to vertex
v
.
Analogously, let
\overline{\delta}
be

\overline{\delta}(a\tau)=\sum_{v\in V(a\tau)}\overline{\delta}_{v}(a\tau).

where
\overline{\delta}_{v}
removes a free edge at vertex
v
, and returns zero if
v
has no free edge.

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\overline{\Phi}
be the extension of the fertility map
\Phi
on
\overline{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}}
defined as

\displaystyle\Phi:\overline{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\overline{a^{1}}\cdots\overline{a^{n}}\,\overline{t}

\displaystyle\mapsto\overline{\Phi}(\overline{a^{1}})\odot\cdots\odot\Phi(\overline{a^{n}})\odot\overline{\Phi}(\overline{t})

where

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}(\overline{t})=\prod_{v\in V(\overline{t})}x_{f(v)-1}^{d(v)},\quad\overline{\Phi}(\overline{a^{j}})=\prod_{v\in V(a^{j})}x_{f(v)-1}^{d(v)}.

Analogously, formula (
3.5
) allows to extend the map
j^{aro}
into

\displaystyle\overline{j^{aro}}:\overline{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}},

where the pairing is extended naturally to
\overline{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{F}}
, where the symmetry factor of aromatic forest with free edges is the symmetry factor of the corresponding
\mathcal{C}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
decorated aromatic forest.

Proposition 4.8
.

The map
\delta
is the transpose of
\overline{\delta}
, that is, for
\tau^{1},\tau^{2}\in\overline{A_{0}}
or
\tau^{1},\tau^{2}\in\overline{T}
,

\langle\delta\tau^{1},\tau^{2}\rangle=\langle\tau^{1},\overline{\delta}\tau^{2}\rangle.

Moreover,
\partial
and
\delta
satisfy the following identity on
\overline{A_{0}}
and
\overline{T}
:

\overline{\Phi}\circ\delta=\partial\circ\overline{\Phi}.

Proof.

Choosing a vertex
v
(resp.
w
) in an aroma
a
(resp.
b
), and considering the sets

P:=\{v\in V(a),\delta_{v}(a)=b\},\quad Q:=\{w\in V(b),\overline{\delta_{w}}(b)=a\},

we find by the orbit-stabiliser theorem

P\simeq Aut(a)/Aut^{v}(a),\quad Q\simeq Aut(b)/Aut^{w}(b).

where
v
(resp.
w
) is any choice of element in
P
(resp.
Q
), and where
Aut^{v}(a)
is the stabiliser of
v
in
Aut(a)
(and similarly for
Q
). If two trees
a
and
b
are such that
b=\delta_{v}(a)
for some
v\in V(a)
, then both sets of vertices
V(a)
and
V(b)
can be naturally identified. In that case, from the natural isomorphism
Aut^{v}(a)\simeq Aut^{v}(b)
, we obtain

\sigma(b)|P|=\sigma(a)|Q|.

We therefore have

\displaystyle\langle\delta a,b\rangle

\displaystyle=|P|\sigma(b)

\displaystyle=|Q|\sigma(a)

\displaystyle=\langle a,\overline{\delta}b\rangle.

which proves the first assertion on
\overline{A_{0}}
. The proof is similar on
\overline{T}
.

\square

The transpose of the fertility identity in Proposition
4.8
yields the following.

Corollary 4.9
.

The following identity holds on
\overline{M_{0}}
and
\overline{M_{-1}}
:

\overline{\delta}\circ\overline{j^{aro}}=\overline{j^{aro}}\circ\overline{\partial}.

Proposition 4.10
.

let
a\tau
be an aroma tree with
r
free edges. Let
r_{v}
be the number of the free edges at the vertex
v
of
a\tau
. Then we have

\overline{\delta}^{r}(a\tau)=\frac{r!}{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}(a\tau)}r_{v}!}a_{0}\tau_{0},

where
a_{0}\tau_{0}
is the aromatic tree with all free edges removed. For aromas only, one finds

\overline{\delta}^{r}(a)=\frac{r!}{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}(a)}r_{v}!}a_{0}.

Proof.

We have

\overline{\delta}^{r}(a\tau)=\sum_{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})\in\mathcal{V}(a\tau)^{r}}\overline{\delta}_{v_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\overline{\delta}_{v_{r}}(a\tau).

A term in the right-hand side is equal to
a_{0}
if and only if each vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}(a\tau)
appears exactly
r_{v}
times in the tuple
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})
, otherwise the term is equal to zero. The number of such
r
-tuples is equal to the multi-monomial coefficient above.

\square

For any admissible cut
c
of an aromatic tree
a\tau\in\mathcal{A}T
, let
\overline{R}_{c}(a\tau)
be the associated full trunk, which is given by the trunk
R_{c}(a\tau)
together with each cut edges replaced by a free edge. The weight of the full trunk is equal to the number of edges belonging to the cut.
In view of Proposition
4.10
, we have

\overline{\delta}^{r}\big(\overline{R}^{c}(a\tau)\big)=\|\overline{a}\,\overline{\tau}\|R^{c}(a\tau),\quad\|\overline{a}\,\overline{\tau}\|:=\frac{r!}{\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}(\overline{a}\,\overline{\tau})}r_{v}!},

(4.1)

where
\overline{a}\,\overline{\tau}
is the shorthand for
\overline{R}^{c}(a\tau)
.

4.2
Combinatorics of the graftings and cuts

Definition 4.11
.

Let
r
be a positive integer, let
A
be an aromatic forest with
r
connected components without free edges, and let
\overline{a}
be an aromatic tree with
r
free edges. A grafting of
A
on
\overline{a}
consists in a a given way to attach the
r
roots in
A
to the
r
free edges in
\overline{a}
, removing the free edges in the operation. The set of all possible graftings of
A
on
\overline{a}
is denoted
\mathcal{G}(A,\overline{a})
and satisfies

|\mathcal{G}(A,\overline{a})|=\|\overline{a}\|.

(4.2)

For a vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}(\overline{a})
and a grafting
b\in\mathcal{G}(A,\overline{a})
, we denote by
F_{b}(v)
the subforest of
A
attached to
v
via
b
.

Examples of admissible cuts are presented below. It should be noted that an admissible cut for aroma cannot cut loops:

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 26.08 11.02 C 26.08 12.03 25.26 12.85 24.25 12.85 C 23.24 12.85 22.43 12.03 22.43 11.02 C 22.43 10.01 23.24 9.2 24.25 9.2 C 25.26 9.2 26.08 10.01 26.08 11.02 Z M 24.25 11.02}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

For the choices of admissible cuts and graftings on rooted part, we have the following result, adapted from
[
37
]
for aromas.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
r
be a positive integer, let
A
and
\overline{a}
as in Definition
4.11
and let
at
be an aromatic tree without free edges. Let
\mathcal{G}(a,A,\overline{a})
be the set of graftings of
A
on
\overline{a}
resulting in the tree
a
. Let
\mathcal{C}(a,A,\overline{a})
be the set of admissible cuts of
a
such that
P^{c}(a)=A
and
\overline{R}^{c}(a)=\overline{a}
. Then, one has

|\mathcal{C}(a,A,\overline{a})|=\frac{\sigma(a)}{\sigma(A)\sigma(\overline{a})}|\mathcal{G}(a,A,\overline{a})|.

Proof.

The group
\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits a
acts transitively on
\mathcal{C}(a,A,\overline{a})
. The stabiliser of a cut
c
will be denoted by
\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{c}a\tau
and its cardinal by
\sigma_{c}(a)
. On the other hand, the group
\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut }}\nolimits\overline{a}\times\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits A
acts transitively on
\mathcal{G}(a,A,\overline{a})
. To see this, consider two graftings
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}(a,A,\overline{a})
: there exists a permutation
\alpha
of
\mathcal{V}(\overline{a})
such that
F_{b}(x)
and
F_{b^{\prime}}\big(\alpha(x)\big)
are isomorphic, and the permutation
\alpha
necessarily comes from an automorphism of the tree
a
. The stabiliser of
b
is
\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}\overline{a}\times\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}A
, where
\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}\overline{a}
is the subgroup of those
\alpha\in\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits\overline{a}
such that
F_{b}\big(\alpha(x)\big)
and
F_{b}(x)
are isomorphic for any vertex
x
of
\overline{a}
, and where
\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}A=\prod_{x\in\mathcal{V}(\overline{a})}\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}(x)
is the subgroup
\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits A
which respects the subforests
F_{b}(x)
. By the orbit-stabiliser theorem, we therefore have

\frac{|\mathcal{C}(a,A,\overline{a})|}{|\mathcal{G}(a,A,\overline{a})|}=\frac{\sigma(a)}{|\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{c}(a)|}\frac{|\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}(\overline{a})|.|\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}(A)|}{\sigma(A)\sigma(\overline{a})}

We conclude by noticing the natural isomorphism
\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{c}(a)\sim\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}(\overline{a})\times\mathop{\hbox{\rm Aut}}\nolimits_{b}(A)
.

\square

4.3
The aromatic LOT coproduct

Let us provide an alternative definition of the aromatic LOT coproduct and show that is it equivalent to the one given in Theorem
3.3
.

Definition 4.13
.

An admissible cut
\mathbf{c}
of a monomial
x^{\kappa}\in M_{0}
is a tuple of the form
(\mathbf{k}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{k}^{r},\overline{\kappa})
that satisfies

\kappa=\mathbf{k}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{k}^{r}+\overline{\kappa},\quad\mathbf{wt}(x^{\mathbf{k}^{j}})=-1.

Analogously, an admissible cut
\mathbf{c}
of a monomial
x^{\mathbf{k}}\in M_{-1}
is a tuple of the form
(\mathbf{k}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{k}^{r},\overline{\mathbf{k}})
that satisfies

\mathbf{k}=\mathbf{k}^{1}+\cdots+\mathbf{k}^{r}+\overline{\mathbf{k}},\quad\mathbf{wt}(x^{\mathbf{k}^{j}})=-1.

We associate to an admissible cut on
M_{0}
the following maps (and analogously on
M_{-1}
),

P^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa})=x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{r}},\quad R^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa})=\overline{\partial}^{r}x^{\overline{\kappa}}.

The LOT coproduct is given on
M_{0}
and
M_{-1}
by

\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\kappa})=x^{\kappa}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in Adm(x^{\kappa})}P^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa})\otimes R^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}),\quad\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\mathbf{k}})=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in Adm(x^{\mathbf{k}})}P^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\mathbf{k}})\otimes R^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\mathbf{k}}).

Then,
\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}:\mathcal{A}M_{-1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
is defined by (
3.4
).

Example 4.14
.

One finds for the monomial
x_{-1}^{2}x_{1}^{2}
that

\displaystyle\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x_{-1}^{2}x_{1}^{2})=

\displaystyle x_{-1}^{2}x_{1}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{-1}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+2x_{-1}\otimes\overline{\partial}(x_{-1}x_{1}^{2})+2x_{-1}^{2}x_{1}\otimes\overline{\partial}x_{1}

\displaystyle+x_{-1}\cdot x_{-1}\otimes\overline{\partial}^{2}(x_{1}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{-1}^{2}x_{1}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{-1}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+4x_{-1}\otimes x_{-1}x_{0}x_{1}+2x_{-1}^{2}x_{1}\otimes x_{0}

\displaystyle+2x_{-1}\cdot x_{-1}\otimes x_{-1}x_{1}+2x_{-1}\cdot x_{-1}\otimes x_{0}^{2}

Let us denote by
|\mathbf{c}|
the set of admissible cuts
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}
such that
P^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=P^{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})
(and therefore
R^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=R^{\mathbf{c}^{\prime}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})
), and by
\|\mathbf{c}\|
the cardinal of this class. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{\|}\mathbf{c}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa!}{\overline{\kappa}!\sigma(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}})}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{k}!}{\overline{\mathbf{k}!}\sigma(\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{t}})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa!}{\overline{\kappa}\kappa^{1}!\cdots\kappa^{r}!\sigma^{ext}(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}})}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{k}!}{\overline{\mathbf{k}}\mathbf{k}^{1}!\cdots\mathbf{k}^{t}!\sigma^{ext}(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{t}})}

whenever
P^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{t}}
, where
\sigma^{ext}
is the external symmetry factor. In analogy with trees, the full trunk is defined by

\overline{R}^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}}):=x^{\overline{\kappa}}\odot x^{\overline{\mathbf{k}}}.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
\mathbf{c}
be an admissible cut of the monomial
x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}}
, and let
c
be an admissible cut of aroma tree
at
. We say that
c
matches
\mathbf{c}
and write
c\sim\mathbf{c}
whenever

•

x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}}=\Phi(a)\Phi(\tau),

•

P^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=\Phi\big(P^{c}(a\tau)\big)

An admissible cut
c
matches
\mathbf{c}
if and only if it matches any element
\mathbf{c}^{\prime}\in|c|
.

Note that the second condition implies
\overline{R}^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=\Phi\big(\overline{R}^{c}(a\tau)\big)
. Therefore, we have
P^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})=\Phi(P^{c}(a\tau))=\Phi(P^{c}(a))\Phi(P^{c}(\tau))=P^{c}(x^{\kappa})\odot P^{c}(x^{\mathbf{k}})
, and analogously for
\overline{R}^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})
. Then, Theorem
3.3
is a straightforward consequence of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.16
.

For any monomial
x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}}
in
\mathcal{\mathcal{H}}_{LOT}^{aro}
and for any admissible cut
\mathbf{c}
of
x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}}
, the following holds:

\|\mathbf{c}\|(j\otimes j)\big(P^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})\otimes R^{\mathbf{c}}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})\big)=\sum_{a\tau,\Phi(a\tau)=x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}}}\sum_{c\in Adm(a\tau),c\sim\mathbf{c}}\frac{\sigma(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})}{\sigma(a\tau)}P^{c}(a\tau)\otimes R^{c}(a\tau).

(4.3)

Proof.

Fix an admissible cut
\mathbf{c}
of the monomial
x^{\kappa}
. Denoting the left-hand side and the right-hand side of (
4.3
) by
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
respectively.
A computation yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa!}{\kappa^{1}!\cdots\kappa^{r}!\overline{\kappa}!\sigma^{ext}(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}})}j(x^{\kappa^{1}})\cdots j(x^{\kappa^{r}})\otimes j\circ\overline{\partial}^{r}(x^{\overline{\kappa}})

\displaystyle=(\Id\otimes\overline{\delta}^{r})\Big(\frac{\kappa!}{\kappa^{1}!\cdots\kappa^{r}!\overline{\kappa}!\sigma^{ext}(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}})}j(x^{\kappa^{1}})\cdots j(x^{\kappa^{r}})\otimes j(x^{\overline{\kappa}})\Big)

\displaystyle(\text{from Corollary }\ref{deltabar-j})

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa!}{\kappa^{1}!\cdots\kappa^{r}!\overline{\kappa}!\sigma^{ext}(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}})}(\Id\otimes\overline{\delta}^{r})\left(\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{(a^j)_{1,\ldots, r,}}{\Phi(a^j)=\mathbf x^{\mathbf\kappa^j} }}\sum_{\overline{a},\Phi(\overline{a})=x^{\overline{\kappa}}}\frac{\kappa^{1}!}{\sigma(a^{1})}\cdots\frac{\kappa^{r}!}{\sigma(a^{r})}\frac{\overline{\kappa}}{\sigma(\overline{a})}\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa!(\Id\otimes\overline{\delta}^{r})\left(\sum_{A,\Phi(A)=x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}}}\sum_{\overline{a},\Phi(\overline{a})=x^{\overline{\kappa}}}\frac{1}{\sigma(A)\sigma(\overline{a})}A\otimes\overline{a}\right).

From equations (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) and Proposition
4.12
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\kappa!\sum_{A,\Phi(A)=x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}}}\sum_{\overline{a},\Phi(\overline{a})=x^{\overline{\kappa}}}\|\overline{a}\|\frac{1}{\sigma(A)\sigma(\overline{a})}A\otimes\overline{a_{0}}

\displaystyle=\kappa!\sum_{A,\Phi(A)=x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}}}\sum_{\overline{a},\Phi(\overline{a})=x^{\overline{\kappa}}}\frac{|\mathcal{G}(A,\overline{a})|}{\sigma(A)\sigma(\overline{a})}A\otimes\overline{a}_{0}

\displaystyle=\kappa!\sum_{a,\Phi(a)=x^{\kappa}}\sum_{A,\Phi(A)=x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}}}\sum_{\overline{a},\Phi(\overline{a})=x^{\overline{\kappa}}}\frac{|\mathcal{G}(a,A,\overline{a})|}{\sigma(A)\sigma(\overline{a})}A\otimes\overline{a}_{0}

\displaystyle=\kappa!\sum_{a,\Phi(a)=x^{\kappa}}\sum_{A,\Phi(A)=x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{r}}}\sum_{\overline{a},\Phi(\overline{a})=x^{\overline{\kappa}}}\frac{|\mathcal{C}(a,A,\overline{a})|}{\sigma(a)}A\otimes\overline{a}_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,\Phi(a)=x^{\kappa}}\frac{\kappa!}{\sigma(a)}\sum_{c\in Adm(a),c\sim\mathbf{c}}P^{c}(a)\otimes R^{c}(a)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}.

We obtain the expression of
\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}
on
M_{0}
and
M_{-1}
. Then, as the coproduct
\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}
is a morphism for
\odot
, we have

\displaystyle(j\otimes j)\big(\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}})\big)

\displaystyle=(j\otimes j)\big(\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\kappa})\odot\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}(x^{\mathbf{k}})\big)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}\big(j(x^{\kappa})\big)\odot\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}\big(j(x^{\mathbf{k}})\big).

This yields the identity (
4.3
).

\square

Let us now study the links between
j^{cl}
and
j^{aro}
to prove Theorem
3.10
.

Lemma 4.17
.

The maps
j^{cl}
and
j^{aro}
satisfy the identity

j^{cl}\circ\varphi^{*}=\psi^{*}\circ j^{aro}.

Proof.

We choose any element
\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{z}=x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\dots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\dots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}\in\mathcal{H}_{LOT}^{aro}
, it can also be written as
\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot\dots\odot\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\dots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}
, here we should note that
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot\dots\odot\mathbf{y}^{n}
and
\mathbf{y}^{i},1\leq i\leq n
which satisfies
\Phi(\mathbf{r}^{i})=\mathbf{y}^{i}
, where we write multi-aromas by
\mathbf{r}^{i}
and aromas by
r^{i}
.
Then, a computation yields

\displaystyle\psi^{*}

\displaystyle\circ j^{aro}(\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{z})=\psi^{*}\circ j^{aro}(x^{\kappa^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\kappa^{m}}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\big((\sum_{\Phi(x^{\kappa^{1}})=r^{1}}\frac{\sigma(x^{\kappa^{1}})}{\sigma(r^{1})}r^{1})\cdots(\sum_{\Phi(x^{\kappa^{m}})=r^{m}}\frac{\sigma(x^{\kappa^{m}})}{\sigma(r^{m})}r^{m})

\displaystyle\cdot(\sum_{\Phi(\tau^{1})=x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}}\frac{\sigma(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})}{\sigma(\tau^{1})}\tau^{1})\cdots(\sum_{\Phi(\tau^{1})=x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}}}\frac{\sigma(x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})}{\sigma(\tau^{n})}\tau^{n})\big)

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\big(\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{(r^i)_{1,\ldots, m, }(\tau^j)_{1, \ldots, n}}{\Phi(r^i)=\mathbf x^{\mathbf\kappa^i}, \Phi(\tau^j)=x^{\mathbf k^j}}}\frac{\sigma(x^{\kappa^{1}})}{\sigma(r^{1})}r^{1}\cdots\frac{\sigma(x^{\kappa^{m}})}{\sigma(r^{m})}r^{m}\cdot\frac{\sigma(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})}{\sigma(\tau^{1})}\tau^{1}\cdots\frac{\sigma(x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})}{\sigma(\tau^{n})}\tau^{n}\big)

\displaystyle=\psi^{*}\big(\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{(\mathbf\mathbf r^i)_{1,\ldots, n, }(\tau^j)_{1, \ldots, n}}{\Phi(\mathbf r^i)=\mathbf y^i, \Phi(\tau^j)=x^{\mathbf k^j}}}\frac{\sigma^{ext}(\mathbf{y}^{1})\sigma^{int}(\mathbf{y}^{1})}{\sigma^{ext}(\mathbf{r}^{1})\sigma^{int}(\mathbf{r}^{1})}\mathbf{r}^{1}\cdots\frac{\sigma^{ext}(\mathbf{y}^{n})\sigma^{int}(\mathbf{y}^{n})}{\sigma^{ext}(\mathbf{r}^{n})\sigma^{int}(\mathbf{r}^{n})}\mathbf{r}^{n}\cdot\frac{\sigma(x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})}{\sigma(\tau^{1})}\tau^{1}\cdots\frac{\sigma(x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})}{\sigma(\tau^{n})}\tau^{n}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{(\mathbf r^i)_{1,\ldots, n }}{\Phi(\mathbf r^1)\cdots\Phi(\mathbf r^n)=\mathbf y^1\odot\cdots\odot\mathbf y^n}}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{(\mathbf r^i)_{1,\ldots, n, }(\tau^j)_{1, \ldots, n}}{\Phi(\mathbf r^i \tau^j)=\mathbf y^i\odot x^{\mathbf k^j}}}(\frac{\sigma(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})}{\sigma(\mathbf{r}^{1}\tau^{1})}\mathbf{r}^{1}\tau^{1})\cdots(\frac{\sigma(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})}{\sigma(\mathbf{r}^{n}\tau^{n})}\mathbf{r}^{n}\tau^{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{(\mathbf r^i)_{1,\ldots, n, }(\tau^j)_{1, \ldots, n}}{\Phi(\mathbf r^i \tau^j)=\mathbf y^i\odot x^{\mathbf k^j}}}\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{(\mathbf r^i)_{1,\ldots, n }}{\Phi(\mathbf r^1)\cdots\Phi(\mathbf r^n)=\mathbf y^1\odot\cdots\odot\mathbf y^n}}(\frac{\sigma(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})}{\sigma(\mathbf{r}^{1}\tau^{1})}\mathbf{r}^{1}\tau^{1})\cdots(\frac{\sigma(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})}{\sigma(\mathbf{r}^{n}\tau^{n})}\mathbf{r}^{n}\tau^{n})

\displaystyle=j^{cl}\big(\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{2}{(\mathbf r^i)_{1,\ldots, n }}{\Phi(\mathbf r^1)\cdots\Phi(\mathbf r^n)=\mathbf y^1\odot\cdots\odot\mathbf y^n}}(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}})\diamond\cdots\diamond(\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})\big)

\displaystyle=j^{cl}\circ\varphi^{*}(\mathbf{y}^{1}\odot\cdots\odot\mathbf{y}^{n}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{1}}\odot\cdots\odot x^{\mathbf{k}^{n}})

\displaystyle=j^{cl}\circ\varphi^{*}(\mathbf{y}\odot\mathbf{z})

Hence the result.

\square

Proof of the embeddings of Theorem
3.10
.

By applying Lemma
4.16
and summing over all classes
|\mathbf{c}|
of admissible cuts
\mathbf{c}
of the monomial
x^{\kappa}\odot x^{\mathbf{k}}
, the coproduct
\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}
verifies on
\mathcal{A}\mathcal{M}
:

(j^{aro}\otimes j^{aro})\circ\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}=\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}\circ j^{aro}.

This implies that on
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle(j^{cl}\otimes j^{cl})\circ\Delta_{LOT}^{cl}

\displaystyle=(j^{cl}\otimes j^{cl})\circ(\varphi^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\circ\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}

\displaystyle=(\psi^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\circ(j^{aro}\otimes j^{cl})\circ\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}

\displaystyle=(\psi^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\circ(j^{aro}\otimes j^{aro})\circ\Delta_{LOT}^{aro}

\displaystyle=(\psi^{*}\otimes\mathds{1})\circ\Delta_{BCK}^{aro}\circ j^{aro}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{BCK}^{cl}\circ j^{cl}

where we used that
j^{aro}
and
j^{cl}
coincide on
\mathcal{A}M_{-1}
and Lemma
4.17
.

\square
